\displaystyle-\sum_{i}b^{i}\dot{\Upsilon}\left(\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}}\frac{\partial f_{\Upsilon}}{\partial\Upsilon}\hat{\psi}_{i},\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle+\langle\frac{\partial\hat{\psi}_{i}}{\partial\Upsilon},\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle\right)+\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}E}C[f],\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle\,,

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the inner product defined by the weight function, Eq. (
C.31
),

\langle h_{1},h_{2}\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}h_{1}(z)h_{2}(z)w_{\Upsilon}(z)dz\,.

(C.40)

The collision term contains polynomial nonlinearities when multiple coupled distribution are being modeled using a
2
-
2
collision operator, Eq. (
C.15
), while the other terms are linear.

To isolate the linear part, we define matrices

\displaystyle A^{k}_{i}(\Upsilon)\equiv

\displaystyle\langle\frac{z}{f_{\Upsilon}}\hat{\psi}_{i}\partial_{z}f_{\Upsilon},\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle+\langle z\partial_{z}\hat{\psi}_{i},\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle\,,

(C.41)

\displaystyle B^{k}_{i}(\Upsilon)\equiv

\displaystyle C_{i}^{k}(\Upsilon)+D_{i}^{k}(\Upsilon),\hskip 5.69054ptC_{i}^{k}\equiv\Upsilon\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}}\frac{\partial f_{\Upsilon}}{\partial\Upsilon}\hat{\psi}_{i},\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle,\hskip 5.69054ptD_{i}^{k}\equiv\Upsilon\langle\frac{\partial\hat{\psi}_{i}}{\partial\Upsilon},\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle\,.

With these definitions, the equations for the
b^{k}
become

\displaystyle\dot{b}^{k}=

\displaystyle\left(H+\frac{\dot{T}}{T}\right)\sum_{i}A_{i}^{k}(\Upsilon)b^{i}-\frac{\dot{\Upsilon}}{\Upsilon}\sum_{i}B_{i}^{k}(\Upsilon)b^{i}+\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}E}C[f],\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle\,.

(C.42)

See Sec.
C.1
for details on how to recursively construct the
\partial_{z}\hat{\psi}_{i}
. We also showed how to compute the inner products
\langle\hat{\psi}_{k},\partial_{\Upsilon}\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle
.
In Eq. (
C.9
)-Eq. (
C.13
) we proved that that both
A
and
B
are lower triangular and show that the only inner products involving the
\partial_{\Upsilon}\hat{\psi}_{i}
that are required in order to compute
A
and
B
are those the above mentioned diagonal elements,
\langle\hat{\psi}_{k},\partial_{\Upsilon}\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle
.

We fix the dynamics of
T
and
\Upsilon
by imposing the conditions

b^{0}(t)\hat{\psi}_{0}(t)=1\,,\hskip 5.69054ptb^{1}(t)=0\,.

(C.43)

In other words,

f(t,z)=f_{\Upsilon}(t,z)\left(1+\phi(t,z)\right),\hskip 5.69054pt\phi=\sum_{i=2}^{N}b^{i}\hat{\psi}_{i}\,.

(C.44)

This reduces the number of degrees of freedom in Eq. (
C.42
) from
N+3
to
N+1
. In other words, after enforcing Eq. (
C.43
), Eq. (
C.42
) constitutes
N+1
equations for the remaining
N+1
unknowns,
b^{2},...,b^{N}
,
\Upsilon
, and
T
. We will call
T
and
\Upsilon
the first two “modes”, as their dynamics arise from imposing the conditions Eq. (
C.43
) on the zeroth and first order coefficients in the expansion. We will solve for their dynamics explicitly below.

To see the physical motivation for the choices Eq. (
C.43
), consider the particle number density and energy density. Using the orthonormality of the
\hat{\psi}_{i}
and Eq. (
C.43
) we have

\displaystyle n=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{p}T^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{i}b^{i}\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\Upsilon}\hat{\psi}_{i}z^{2}dz=\frac{g_{p}T^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{i}b^{i}\langle\hat{\psi}_{i},1\rangle

(C.45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{p}T^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}b^{0}\langle\hat{\psi}_{0},1\rangle=\frac{g_{p}T^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\langle 1,1\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\rho=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{p}T^{4}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{i}b^{i}\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\Upsilon}\hat{\psi}_{i}z^{3}dz=\frac{g_{p}T^{4}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{i}b^{i}\langle\hat{\psi}_{i},z\rangle

(C.46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{p}T^{4}}{2\pi^{2}}\left(b^{0}\langle\hat{\psi}_{0},z\rangle+b^{1}\langle\hat{\psi}_{1},z\rangle\right)=\frac{g_{p}T^{4}}{2\pi^{2}}\langle 1,z\rangle\,.

These, together with the definition of the weight function, Eq. (
C.31
), imply

\displaystyle n=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{p}T^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\Upsilon}z^{2}dz\,,

(C.47)

\displaystyle\rho=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{p}T^{4}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\Upsilon}z^{3}dz\,.

(C.48)

Equations (
C.47
) and (
C.48
) show that the first two modes,
T
and
\Upsilon
, with time evolution fixed by Eq. (
C.43
) cause the chemical nonequilibrium distribution
f_{\Upsilon}
to capture the number density and energy density of the system exactly. This fact is very significant, as it implies that within the chemical nonequilibrium approach as long as the back-reaction from the non-thermal distortions is small (meaning that the evolution of
T(t)
and
\Upsilon(t)
is not changed significantly when more modes are included),
all the effects relevant to the computation of particle and energy flow are modeled by the time evolution of
T
and
\Upsilon
alone
and no further modes are necessary. This gives a clear separation between the averaged physical quantities, characterized by
f_{\Upsilon}
, and the momentum dependent non-thermal distortions as captured by

\phi=\sum_{i=2}^{N}b^{i}\hat{\psi}_{i}\,.

(C.49)

One should contrast this chemical nonequilibrium behavior with the chemical equilibrium method, where a minimum of four modes is required to describe the number and energy densities, as shown in Eq. (
C.28
). Moreover we will show that convergence to the desired precision is faster in the chemical nonequilibrium approach as compared to chemical equilibrium. Due to the high cost of numerically integrating realistic collision integrals of the form Eq. (
C.15
), this fact can be very significant in applications. We remark that the relations Eq. (
C.47
) are the physical motivation for including the
z^{2}
factor in the weight function. All three modifications we have made in constructing our new method, the introduction of an effective temperature, i.e.,
R\neq 1
, the generalization to chemical nonequilibrium
f_{\Upsilon}
, and the introduction of
z^{2}
to the weight, Eq. (
C.36
), were needed to obtain the properties, Eq. (
C.47
), but it is the introduction of
z^{2}
that reduces the number of required modes and hence reduces the computational cost.

With
b^{0}
and
b^{1}
fixed as in Eq. (
C.43
) we can solve the equations for
\dot{b}^{0}
and
\dot{b}^{1}
from Eq. (
C.42
) for
\dot{\Upsilon}
and
\dot{T}
to obtain

\displaystyle\dot{\Upsilon}/{\Upsilon}=

\displaystyle\frac{(Ab)^{1}\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}E}C[f],\hat{\psi}_{0}\rangle-(Ab)^{0}\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}E}C[f],\hat{\psi}_{1}\rangle}{[\Upsilon\partial_{\Upsilon}\langle 1,1\rangle/(2||\psi_{0}||)+(Bb)^{0}](Ab)^{1}-(Ab)^{0}(Bb)^{1}}\,,

(C.50)

\displaystyle\dot{T}/T=

\displaystyle\frac{(Bb)^{1}\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}E}C[f],\hat{\psi}_{0}\rangle-\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}E}C[f],\hat{\psi}_{1}\rangle[\Upsilon\partial_{\Upsilon}\langle 1,1\rangle/(2||\psi_{0}||)+(Bb)^{0}]}{[\Upsilon\partial_{\Upsilon}\langle 1,1\rangle/(2||\psi_{0}||)+(Bb)^{0}](Ab)^{1}-(Ab)^{0}(Bb)^{1}}-H

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(Ab)^{1}}\left((Bb)^{1}\dot{\Upsilon}/\Upsilon-\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}E}C[f],\hat{\psi}_{1}\rangle\right)-H\,.

(C.51)

Here
(Ab)^{n}=\sum_{j=0}^{N}A^{n}_{j}b^{j}
and similarly for
B
and
||\cdot||
is the norm induced by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. In deriving this, we used

\dot{b}^{0}=\frac{1}{2||\psi_{0}||}\dot{\Upsilon}\partial_{\Upsilon}\langle 1,1\rangle\,,\hskip 11.38109pt\partial_{\Upsilon}\langle 1,1\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{2}}{(e^{z/2}+\Upsilon e^{-z/2})^{2}}dz\,,

(C.52)

which comes from differentiating Eq. (
C.43
).

It is easy to check that when the collision operator vanishes, then the above system is solved by

\Upsilon=\text{constant}\,,\hskip 11.38109pt\frac{\dot{T}}{T}=-H\,,\hskip 5.69054ptb^{n}=\text{constant}\,,\hskip 2.84526ptn>2\,,

(C.53)

i.e., the fugacity and non-thermal distortions are ‘frozen’ into the distribution and the temperature satisfies dilution scaling
T\propto 1/a
.

When the collision term becomes small, Eq. (
C.53
) motivates another change of variables. Letting
T=(1+\epsilon)/a
gives the equation

\dot{\epsilon}=\frac{1+\epsilon}{(Ab)^{1}}\left((Bb)^{1}\dot{\Upsilon}/\Upsilon-\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}E}C[f],\hat{\psi}_{1}\rangle\right).

(C.54)

Solving this in place of Eq. (
C.51
) when the collision terms are small avoids having to numerically track the free-streaming evolution. In particular this will ensure conservation of comoving particle number, which equals a function of
\Upsilon
multiplied by
(aT)^{3}
, to much greater precision in this regime as well as resolve the freeze-out temperatures more accurately.

Projected Dynamics are Well-defined:

The following calculation shows that, for a distribution initially in kinetic equilibrium, the determinant factor in the denominator of Eq. (
C.50
) is nonzero and hence the dynamics for
T
and
\Upsilon
, as well as the remainder of the projected system, are well-defined, at least for sufficiently small times.

Kinetic equilibrium implies the initial conditions
b^{0}=||\psi_{0}||
,
b^{i}=0
,
i>0
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle K\equiv

\displaystyle(\Upsilon\partial_{\Upsilon}\langle 1,1\rangle/(2||\psi_{0}||)+(Bb)^{0})(Ab)^{1}-(Ab)^{0}(Bb)^{1}

(C.55)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(C^{0}_{0}A^{1}_{0}-A^{0}_{0}C^{1}_{0})(b^{0})^{2}+\left[(D^{0}_{0}A^{1}_{0}-A^{0}_{0}D^{1}_{0})(b^{0})^{2}+\Upsilon\partial_{\Upsilon}\langle 1,1\rangle/(2||\psi_{0}||)A^{1}_{0}b^{0}\right]

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle K_{1}+K_{2}\,.

\displaystyle K_{1}=

\displaystyle\langle\frac{1}{1+\Upsilon e^{-z}},1\rangle\langle\frac{-z}{1+\Upsilon e^{-z}}\hat{\psi}_{1},\hat{\psi}_{0}\rangle-\langle\frac{-z}{1+\Upsilon e^{-z}},\hat{\psi}_{0}\rangle\langle\frac{1}{1+\Upsilon e^{-z}}\hat{\psi}_{1},1\rangle\,.

(C.56)

Inserting the formula for
\hat{\psi}_{1}
from Eq. (
C.1
), we find

\displaystyle K_{1}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{||\psi_{1}||\,||\psi_{0}||}\left[\langle\frac{1}{1+\Upsilon e^{-z}},\hat{\psi}_{0}\rangle\langle\frac{z^{2}}{1+\Upsilon e^{-z}},\hat{\psi}_{0}\rangle-\langle\frac{z}{1+\Upsilon e^{-z}},\hat{\psi}_{0}\rangle^{2}\right]\,.

(C.57)

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality applied to the inner product with weight function

\tilde{w}=\frac{w}{1+\Upsilon e^{-z}}\hat{\psi}_{0}

(C.58)

together with linear independence of
1
and
z
implies that the term in brackets is positive and so
K_{1}<0
at
t=0
. For the second term, noting that
D^{1}_{0}=0
by orthogonality and using Eq. (
C.7
), we have

\displaystyle K_{2}=

\displaystyle[\langle\partial_{\Upsilon}\hat{\psi}_{0},\hat{\psi}_{0}\rangle||\psi_{0}||+\partial_{\Upsilon}\langle 1,1\rangle/(2||\psi_{0}||)]\Upsilon A_{0}^{1}||\psi_{0}||=0\,.

(C.59)

This proves that
K
is nonzero at
t=0
.

C.3
Validation

We will validate our numerical method on an exactly solvable model problem

\partial_{t}f-pH\partial_{p}f=M\left(\frac{1}{\Upsilon^{-1}e^{p/T_{eq}}+1}-f(p,t)\right)\,,\hskip 5.69054ptf(p,0)=\frac{1}{e^{p/T_{eq}(0)}+1}\,,

(C.60)

where
M
is a constant with units of energy and we choose units in which it is equal to
1
. This model describes a distribution that is attracted to a given equilibrium distribution at a prescribed time dependent temperature
T_{eq}(t)
and fugacity
\Upsilon
. This type of an idealized scattering operator, without fugacity, was first introduced in
[
27
]
. By changing coordinates
y=a(t)p
we find

\partial_{t}f(y,t)=\frac{1}{\Upsilon^{-1}\exp[y/(a(t)T_{eq}(t))]+1}-f(y,t)\,,

(C.61)

which has as solution

f(y,t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{e^{s-t}}{\Upsilon^{-1}\exp[y/(a(s)T_{eq}(s))]+1}ds+\frac{e^{-t}}{\exp[y/(a(0)T_{eq}(0))]+1}\,.

(C.62)

We now transform to
z=p/T(t)
, where the temperature
T
of the distribution
f
is defined as in Sec.
C.2
. Therefore, we have the exact solution to

\partial_{t}f-z\left(H+\frac{\dot{T}}{T}\right)\partial_{z}f=\frac{1}{\Upsilon^{-1}e^{zT/T_{eq}}+1}-f(z,t)\,,

(C.63)

given by

\displaystyle f(z,t)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{e^{s-t}}{\Upsilon^{-1}\exp[a(t)T(t)z/(a(s)T_{eq}(s))]+1}ds

(C.64)

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{-t}}{\exp[a(t)T(t)z/(a(0)T_{eq}(0))]+1}\,.

We use this to test the chemical equilibrium and chemical nonequilibrium methods under two different conditions.

Reheating test

First we compare the chemical equilibrium and nonequilibrium methods in a scenario that exhibits reheating. Motivated by applications to cosmology, we choose a scale factor evolving as in the radiation dominated era, a fugacity
\Upsilon=1
, and choose an equilibrium temperature that exhibits reheating like behavior with
aT_{eq}
increasing for a period of time,

\displaystyle a(t)=\left(\frac{t+b}{b}\right)^{1/2}\,,\ \ \ \ T_{eq}(t)=\frac{1}{a(t)}\left(1+\frac{1-e^{-t}}{e^{-(t-b)}+1}(R-1)\right)\,,

(C.65)

where
R
is the desired reheating ratio. Note that
(aT_{eq})(0)=1
and
(aT_{eq})(t)\rightarrow R
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Qualitatively, this is reminiscent of the dynamics of neutrino freeze-out, but the range of reheating ratio for which we will test our method is larger than found there.

We solved Eq. (
C.61
) and Eq. (
C.63
) numerically using the chemical equilibrium and chemical nonequilibrium methods respectively for
t\in[0,10]
and
b=5
and the cases
R=1.1
,
R=1.4
, as well as the more extreme ratio of
R=2
. The bases of orthogonal polynomials were generated numerically using the recursion relations from Sec.
C.1
. For the applications we are considering, where the solution is a small perturbation of equilibrium, only a small number of terms are required and so the numerical challenges associated with generating a large number of such orthogonal polynomials are not an issue.

Chemical equilibrium method

We solved Eq. (
C.61
) using the chemical equilibrium method, with the orthonormal basis defined by the weight function Eq. (
C.25
) for
N=2,...,10
modes (mode numbers
n=0,...,N-1
) and prescribed single step relative and absolute error tolerances of
10^{-13}
for the numerical integration, and with asymptotic reheating ratios of
R=1.1
,
R=1.4
, and
R=2
and will here compare various aspects of the numerical solution with the exact solution, Eq. (
C.62
).

In Fig.
65

we show the maximum relative error in the number densities and energy densities respectively over the time interval
[0,10]
for various numbers of computed modes. The particle number density and energy density are accurate, up to the integration tolerance level, for
3
or more and
4
or more modes respectively. This is consistent with Eq. (
C.28
), which shows the number of modes required to capture each of these quantities. However, fewer modes than these minimum values lead to a large error in the corresponding moment of the distribution function.

Figure 65:
Maximum relative error, on left-hand side in particle number density and on right-hand side in energy density for various numbers of computed modes. Adapted from Ref.
[
30
]
.

To show that the numerical integration accurately captures the mode coefficients, in Fig.
66
on the left we show the difference between the computed coefficients and actual coefficients, denoted by
\tilde{b}_{n}
and
b_{n}
respectively, as measured by the
L^{\infty}
-error

\text{error}_{L^{\infty}}(n)=\max_{t}|\tilde{b}_{n}(t)-b_{n}(t)|\,.

(C.66)

The error is shown as a function of mode number
n
; here the evolution of the system was computed using a total of
N=10
modes.

Figure 66:
On left: Mode coefficient
L^{\infty}
-error. On right: Maximum ratio of
L^{1}
error between computed and exact solutions to
L^{1}
norm of the exact solution. Adapted from Ref.
[
30
]
.

In Fig.
66
on the right we show the difference between the exact solution
f
, and the numerical solution
f^{N}
computed using a total number of
N=2,...,10
modes, as measured by the maximum relative
L^{1}
error

\text{error}_{L^{1}}(N)=\max_{t}\frac{\int|f-f^{N}|dy}{\int|f|dy}\,.

(C.67)

For
R=1
and
R=1.4
we see that the chemical equilibrium method works reasonably well (as long as the number of modes is at least 4, so that the energy and number densities are properly captured) but for
R=2
the
L^{1}
error is large (see Fig.
66
, right) and the approximate solution exhibits spurious oscillations as seen in Fig.
67
(left). This behavior is expected based on the results we did present earlier in this Appendix.
Further clarifying the behavior, in Fig.
67
on the right-hand side
we show the
L^{1}
error ratio as a function of time for
N=10
modes. In the
R=2
case we see that the error increases as the reheating ratio approaches its asymptotic value of
R=2
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. As we will see, our methods achieves a much higher accuracy for a small number of terms in the case of large reheating ratio due to the replacement of dilution temperature scaling with the dynamical effective temperature
T
.

Figure 67:
On left: Approximate and exact solution for a reheating ratio
R=2
and
N=10
modes. On right:
L^{1}
error ratio as a function of time for
N=10
modes. Adapted from Ref.
[
30
]
.

Chemical non-equilibrium method

We now solve Eq. (
C.61
) using the chemical nonequilibrium method, with the orthonormal basis defined by the weight function, Eq. (
C.31
), for
N=2,...,10
modes, a prescribed numerical integration tolerance of
10^{-13}
, and asymptotic reheating ratios of
R=1.1
,
R=1.4
, and
R=2
and will again compare with the exact solution, Eq. (
C.62
). Recall that we are referring to
T
and
\Upsilon
as the first two modes (
n=0
and
n=1
).

In Fig.
68

we show the maximum relative error over the time interval
[0,10]
in the number densities and energy densities respectively for various numbers of computed modes. Even for only
2
modes, the number and energy densities are accurate up to the integration tolerance level. This is in agreement with the analytical expressions in Eq. (
C.47
).

Figure 68:
Maximum relative error, on left in particle number density and on right in energy density. Adapted from Ref.
[
30
]
.

To show that the numerical integration accurately captures the mode coefficients, in Fig.
69
on left-hand side we show the error Eq. (
C.66
), where the evolution of the system was computed using a total of
N=10
modes. In Fig.
69
on right-hand side we show the error between the approximate and exact solutions, computed as in Eq. (
C.67
) for
N=2,...,10
and
R=1.1
,
R=1.4
, and
R=2
respectively. For most mode numbers and
R
values, the error using
2
modes is substantially less than the error from the chemical equilibrium method using
4
modes. The result is most dramatic for the case of large reheating,
R=2
, where the spurious oscillations from the chemical equilibrium solution are absent in our method, as seen in Fig.
70
on the left-hand side, as compared to the chemical equilibrium method in Fig.
67
on left-hand side. Note that due to the relation
z=y/R
as discussed in before in this Appendix we plot from
z\in[0,15]
in comparison to
y\in[0,30]
in Fig.
70
.

Figure 69:
On left: Maximum error in mode coefficients. On right: Maximum ratio of
L^{1}
error between computed and exact solutions to
L^{1}
norm of the exact solution. Adapted from Ref.
[
30
]
.

Additionally, the error no longer increases as
t\rightarrow\infty
, as it did for the chemical equilibrium method, see Fig.
70
right-hand side.
In fact it decreases since the exact solution approaches chemical equilibrium at a reheated temperature and hence can be better approximated by
f_{\Upsilon}
.

Figure 70:
On left: Approximate and exact solution for
R=2
obtained with two modes. On right:
L^{1}
error ratio as a function of time for
N=10
modes. Adapted from Ref.
[
30
]
.

In summary, in addition to the reduction in the computational cost when going from
4
to
2
modes, our method also reduces the error as compared to the chemical equilibrium method, all while accurately capturing the number and energy densities.

Appendix D
Neutrino Collision Integrals

D.1
Collision integral inner products

Having detailed our method for solving the Boltzmann-Einstein equation in Sec.
C
, we address the computation of collision integrals for neutrino processes; see also
[
46
]
. To solve for the mode coefficients using Eq. (
C.42
), we must evaluate the collision operator inner products

\displaystyle R_{k}\equiv

\displaystyle\langle\frac{1}{f_{\Upsilon}E_{1}}C[f_{1}],\hat{\psi}_{k}\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\hat{\psi}_{k}(z_{1})C[f_{1}](z_{1})\frac{z_{1}^{2}}{E_{1}}dz_{1}

(D.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\hat{\psi}_{k}(z_{1})\int\left[f_{3}(p_{3})f_{4}(p^{4})f^{1}(p_{1})f^{2}(p_{2})-f_{1}(p_{1})f_{2}(p_{2})f^{3}(p_{3})f^{4}(p^{4})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t)(2\pi)^{4}\delta(\Delta p)\prod_{i=2}^{4}\frac{d^{3}p_{i}}{2(2\pi)^{3}E_{i}}\frac{z_{1}^{2}}{E_{1}}dz_{1}\,,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2(2\pi)^{3}}{8\pi}T_{1}^{-3}\int G_{k}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t)(2\pi)^{4}\delta(\Delta p)\prod_{i=1}^{4}\frac{d^{3}p_{i}}{2(2\pi)^{3}E_{i}}\,,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi^{2}T_{1}^{-3}\int G_{k}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t)(2\pi)^{4}\delta(\Delta p)\prod_{i=1}^{4}\delta_{0}(p_{i}^{2}-m_{i}^{2})\frac{d^{4}p_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,,

\displaystyle G_{k}=

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{k}(z_{1})\left[f_{3}(p_{3})f_{4}(p_{4})f^{1}(p_{1})f^{2}(p_{2})-f_{1}(p_{1})f_{2}(p_{2})f^{3}(p_{3})f^{4}(p_{4})\right],\hskip 5.69054ptf^{i}=1-f_{i}\,.

Note that
R_{k}
only uses information about the distributions at a single spacetime point, and so we can work in a local orthonormal basis for the momentum. Among other things, this implies that
p^{2}=p^{\alpha}p^{\beta}\eta_{\alpha\beta}
where
\eta
is the Minkowski metric

\eta_{\alpha\beta}=\mathrm{diag}(1,-1,-1,-1)\,.

(D.2)

From Eq. (
D.1
), we see that a crucial input into the chemical nonequilibrium spectral method with
2\leftrightarrow 2
reactions is the ability to efficiently compute a numerical approximation to integrals of the form

\displaystyle M\equiv\int G(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t)(2\pi)^{4}\delta(\Delta p)\prod_{i=1}^{4}\delta_{0}(p_{i}^{2}-m_{i}^{2})\frac{d^{4}p_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,,

(D.3)

\displaystyle G(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=g_{1}(p_{1})g_{2}(p_{2})g_{3}(p_{3})g_{4}(p_{4})\,,

for some functions
g_{i}
. Even after eliminating the delta functions in Eq. (
D.3
), we are still left with an
8
dimensional integral. To facilitate numerical computation, we must analytically reduce this expression down to fewer dimensions. Fortunately, the systems we are interested in have a large amount of symmetry that can be utilized for this purpose.

The distribution functions we are concerned with are isotropic in some frame defined by a unit timelike vector
U
, i.e. they depend only on the four-momentum only through
p_{i}\cdot U
. The same is true of the basis functions
\hat{\psi}_{k}
, and hence the
g_{i}
depend only on
p_{i}\cdot U
as well. In
[
307
,
308
,
309
]
, approaches are outlined that reduce integrals of this type down to
3
dimensions. We outline the method from
[
308
,
309
]
, as applied to our spectral method solver, in Appendix
D.3
. However, the integrand one obtains from these methods is only piecewise smooth or has an integration domain with a complicated geometry. This presents difficulties for the integration routine we employ, which utilizes adaptive mesh refinement to ensure the desired error tolerance. We take an alternative approach that, for the scattering kernels found in
e^{\pm}
, neutrino interactions, reduces the problem nested integrals of depth three while also resulting in an integrand with better smoothness properties. In our comparison with the method in
[
308
,
309
]
, the resulting formula evaluates significantly faster under the numerical integration scheme we used. The derivation presented here expands on what is found in
[
53
]
.

Simplifying the collision integral

Our strategy for simplifying the collision integrals is as follows. We first make a change of variables designed to put the 4-momentum conserving delta function in a particularly simple form, allowing for the integral to be reduced from
16
to
12
dimensions. The remaining four delta functions, which impose the mass shell constraints, are then seen to reduce to integration over a product of spheres. The simple form of the submanifold that these delta function restrict us to allows us to use the method in chapter
B
to analytically evaluate all four of the remaining delta functions simultaneously. During this process, the isotropy of the system in the frame given by the 4-vector
U
allows for further reduction of the dimensionality by analytically evaluating several of the angular integrals.

The change of variables that simplifies the 4-momentum conserving delta function is given by

p=p_{1}+p_{2}\,,\hskip 5.69054ptq=p_{1}-p_{2}\,,\hskip 5.69054ptp^{\prime}=p_{3}+p_{4}\,,\hskip 5.69054ptq^{\prime}=p_{3}-p_{4}\,.

(D.4)

The Jacobian of this transformation is
1/2^{8}
. Therefore, using Lemma
2
, we find

\displaystyle M=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{256(2\pi)^{8}}\int G((p+q)\cdot U/2,(p-q)\cdot U/2,(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})\cdot U/2,(p^{\prime}-q^{\prime})\cdot U/2)

\displaystyle\times S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}\delta(p-p^{\prime})\delta((p+q)^{2}/4-m_{1}^{2})\delta((p-q)^{2}/4-m_{2}^{2})\delta((p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{3}^{2})

\displaystyle\times\delta((p^{\prime}-q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{4}^{2})1_{p^{0}>|q^{0}|}1_{(p^{\prime})^{0}>|(q^{\prime})^{0}|}d^{4}pd^{4}qd^{4}p^{\prime}d^{4}q^{\prime}\,.

(D.5)

Next, we eliminate the integration over
p^{\prime}
using
\delta(p-p^{\prime})

and then use Fubini’s theorem to write

\displaystyle M=\frac{1}{256(2\pi)^{8}}\int

\displaystyle\bigg[\int G((p+q)\cdot U/2,(p-q)\cdot U/2,(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})\cdot U/2,(p^{\prime}-q^{\prime})\cdot U/2)

\displaystyle\times 1_{p^{0}>|q^{0}|}1_{p^{0}>|(q^{\prime})^{0}|}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}\delta((p+q)^{2}/4-m_{1}^{2})\delta((p-q)^{2}/4-m_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\times\delta((p+q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{3}^{2})\delta((p-q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{4}^{2})d^{4}qd^{4}q^{\prime}\bigg]d^{4}p\,.

(D.6)

Subsequent computations will justify this use of Fubini’s theorem.

Since
p^{0}>0
we have
dp\neq 0
and so we can use Corollary
3
of the coarea formula to decompose this into an integral over the center of mass energy
s=p^{2}
,

\displaystyle M=\frac{1}{256(2\pi)^{8}}\!\int_{s_{0}}^{\infty}\!\!\int\!\bigg[\int 1_{p^{0}>|q^{0}|}1_{p^{0}>|(q^{\prime})^{0}|}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}F(p,q,q^{\prime})\delta((p+q)^{2}/4-m_{1}^{2})

(D.7)

\displaystyle\!\times\delta((p-q)^{2}/4-m_{2}^{2})\delta((p+q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{3}^{2})\delta((p-q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{4}^{2})d^{4}qd^{4}q^{\prime}\bigg]\delta(p^{2}-s)d^{4}pds\,,

\displaystyle F(p,q,q^{\prime})=G((p+q)\cdot U/2,(p-q)\cdot U/2,(p+q^{\prime})\cdot U/2,(p-q^{\prime})\cdot U/2)\,,

\displaystyle s_{0}=\max\{(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2},(m_{3}+m_{4})^{2}\}\,.

The lower bound on
s
comes from the fact that both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are future timelike and hence

p^{2}=m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}+2p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\geq m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}+2m_{1}m_{2}=(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2}\,.

(D.8)

The other inequality is obtained by using
p=p^{\prime}
.

Note that the integral in brackets in Eq. (
D.7
) is invariant under
SO(3)
rotations of
p
in the frame defined by
U
. Therefore we obtain

\displaystyle M=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{256(2\pi)^{8}}\int_{s_{0}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}K(s,p)\frac{4\pi|\vec{p}|^{2}}{2p^{0}}d|\vec{p}|ds\,,\hskip 5.69054ptp^{0}=p\cdot U=\sqrt{|\vec{p}|^{2}+s}\,,

(D.9)

\displaystyle K(s,p)=

\displaystyle\int\!1_{p^{0}>|q^{0}|}1_{p^{0}>|(q^{\prime})^{0}|}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}F(p,q,q^{\prime})\delta((p+q)^{2}/4-m_{1}^{2})\delta((p-q)^{2}/4-m_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\times\delta((p+q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{3}^{2})\delta((p-q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{4}^{2})d^{4}qd^{4}q^{\prime}\,,

where
|\vec{p}|
denotes the norm of the spacial component of
p
and in the formula for
K(s,p)
,
p
is any four vector whose spacial component has norm
|\vec{p}|
and timelike component
\sqrt{|\vec{p}|^{2}+s}
. Note that in integrating over
\delta(p^{2}-s)dp^{0}
, only the positive root was taken, due to the indicator functions in the
K(s,p)
.

We now simplify
K(s,p)
for fixed but arbitrary
p
and
s
that satisfy
p^{0}=\sqrt{|\vec{p}|^{2}+s}
and
s>s_{0}
. These conditions imply
p
is future timelike, hence we can we can change variables in
q,q^{\prime}
by an element of
Q\in SO(1,3)
so that

Qp=(\sqrt{s},0,0,0)\,,\hskip 5.69054ptQU=(\alpha,0,0,\delta)\,,

(D.10)

where

\alpha=\frac{p\cdot U}{\sqrt{s}}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt\delta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}\left((p\cdot U)^{2}-s\right)^{1/2}\,.

(D.11)

Note that the delta functions in the integrand imply
p\pm q
is timelike (or null if the corresponding mass is zero). Therefore
p^{0}>\pm q^{0}
iff
p\mp q
is future timelike (or null). This condition is preserved by
SO(1,3)
hence
p^{0}>|q^{0}|
in one frame iff it holds in every frame. Similar comments apply to
p^{0}>|(q^{\prime})^{0}|
and so
K(s,p)
has the same formula in the transformed frame as well.

We now evaluate the measure that is induced by the delta functions, using the method given in chapter
B
. We have the constraint function

\Phi(q,q^{\prime})=((p+q)^{2}/4-m_{1}^{2},(p-q)^{2}/4-m_{2}^{2},(p+q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{3}^{2},(p-q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{4}^{2})

(D.12)

and must compute the solution set
\Phi(q,q^{\prime})=0
. Adding and subtracting the first two components and the last two respectively, we have the equivalent conditions

\displaystyle\frac{s+q^{2}}{2}=m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}\,,\hskip 5.69054ptp\cdot q=m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt\frac{s+(q^{\prime})^{2}}{2}=m_{3}^{2}+m_{4}^{2}\,,\hskip 5.69054ptp\cdot q^{\prime}=m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2}\,.

(D.13)

If we let
(q^{0},\vec{q})
,
((q^{\prime})^{0},\vec{q}^{\prime})
denote the spacial components in the frame defined by
p=(\sqrt{s},0,0,0)
we have another set of equivalent conditions

\displaystyle q^{0}=\frac{m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt|\vec{q}|^{2}=\frac{(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})^{2}}{s}+s-2(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2})\,,

(D.14)

\displaystyle(q^{\prime})^{0}=\frac{m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt|\vec{q}^{\prime}|^{2}=\frac{(m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})^{2}}{s}+s-2(m_{3}^{2}+m_{4}^{2})\,.

Note that if these hold then using
s\geq s_{0}
, we obtain

\frac{|q^{0}|}{p^{0}}\leq\frac{|m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}|}{(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2}}<1

(D.15)

and similarly for
q^{\prime}
. Hence the conditions in the indicator functions are satisfied and we can drop them from the formula for
K(s,p)
.

The conditions Eq. (
D.14
) imply that our solution set is a product of spheres in
\vec{q}
and
\vec{q}^{\prime}
, as long as the conditions are consistent i.e. so long as
|\vec{q}|,|\vec{q}^{\prime}|>0
. To see that this holds for almost every
s
, first note

\frac{d}{ds}|\vec{q}|^{2}=1-\frac{(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})^{2}}{s^{2}}>0

(D.16)

since
s\geq(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2}
. At
s=(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2}
,
|\vec{q}|^{2}=0
. Therefore, for
s>s_{0}
we have
|\vec{q}|>0
and similarly for
q^{\prime}
. Hence we have the result

\Phi^{-1}(0)=\{q^{0}\}\times B_{|\vec{q}|}\times\{(q^{\prime})^{0}\}\times B_{|\vec{q}^{\prime}|}\,,

(D.17)

where
B_{r}
denotes the radius
r
ball centered at
0
. We will parametrize this by spherical angular coordinates in
q
and
q^{\prime}
.

We now compute the induced volume form. First consider the differential

D\Phi=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{2}(q+p)^{\alpha}\eta_{\alpha\beta}dq^{\beta}\\
\frac{1}{2}(q-p)^{\alpha}\eta_{\alpha\beta}dq^{\beta}\\
\frac{1}{2}(q^{\prime}+p)^{\alpha}\eta_{\alpha\beta}dq^{{}^{\prime\beta}}\\
\frac{1}{2}(q^{\prime}-p)^{\alpha}\eta_{\alpha\beta}dq^{{}^{\prime\beta}}\end{array}\right)\,.

(D.18)

Evaluating this on the coordinate vector fields
\partial_{q^{0}}
,
\partial_{r}
, we obtain

D\Phi(\partial_{q^{0}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{2}(q^{0}+\sqrt{s})\\
\frac{1}{2}(q^{0}-\sqrt{s})\\
0\\
0\end{array}\right),\hskip 5.69054ptD\Phi(\partial_{r})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\frac{1}{2}|\vec{q}|\\
-\frac{1}{2}|\vec{q}|\\
0\\
0\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\frac{1}{2}r\\
-\frac{1}{2}r\\
0\\
0\end{array}\right)\,.

(D.19)

Similar results hold for
q^{\prime}
. Therefore we have the determinant

\det\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}D\Phi(\partial_{q^{0}})&D\Phi(\partial_{r})&D\Phi(\partial_{(q^{\prime})^{0}})&D\Phi(\partial_{r^{\prime}})\end{array}\right)=\frac{s}{4}rr^{\prime}.

(D.20)

Note that this determinant being nonzero implies that our use of Fubini’s theorem in Eq. (
D.6
) was justified.

By Eq. (
B.15
) and Eq. (
B.31
), the above computations imply that the induced volume measure is

\displaystyle\delta((p+q)^{2}/4-m_{1}^{2})\delta((p-q)^{2}/4-m_{2}^{2})\delta((p+q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{3}^{2})\delta((p-q^{\prime})^{2}/4-m_{4}^{2})d^{4}qd^{4}q^{\prime}

(D.21)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{srr^{\prime}}i_{(\partial_{q^{0}},\partial_{r},\partial_{(q^{\prime})^{0}},\partial_{r^{\prime}})}\left(r^{2}\sin(\phi)dq^{0}drd\theta d\phi\right)\wedge\left((r^{\prime})^{2}\sin(\phi^{\prime})d(q^{\prime})^{0}dr^{\prime}d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4rr^{\prime}}{s}\sin(\phi)\sin(\phi^{\prime})d\theta d\phi d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}\,,

where

\displaystyle r=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}\sqrt{(s-(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2})(s-(m_{1}-m_{2})^{2})}\,,

(D.22)

\displaystyle r^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}\sqrt{(s-(m_{3}+m_{4})^{2})(s-(m_{3}-m_{4})^{2})}\,.

Consistent with our interest in the Boltzmann equation, we assume
F
factors as

\displaystyle F(p,q,q^{\prime})=

\displaystyle F_{12}((p+q)\cdot U/2,(p-q)\cdot U/2)F_{34}((p+q^{\prime})\cdot U/2,(p-q^{\prime})\cdot U/2)

(D.23)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle G_{12}(p\cdot U,q\cdot U)G_{34}(p\cdot U,q^{\prime}\cdot U)\,.

For now we suppress the dependence on
p
, as it is not of immediate concern. In our chosen coordinates where
U=(\alpha,0,0,\delta)
, we have

q\cdot U=q^{0}\alpha-r\delta\cos(\phi)

(D.24)

and similarly for
q^{\prime}
.
To compute

\displaystyle K(s,p)=\frac{4rr^{\prime}}{s}\int\left[\int S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t)G_{34}\sin(\phi^{\prime})d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}\right]G_{12}\sin(\phi)d\theta d\phi\,,

(D.25)

first recall

\displaystyle t=

\displaystyle(p_{1}-p_{3})^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(q-q^{\prime})^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(q^{2}+(q^{\prime})^{2}-2(q^{0}(q^{\prime})^{0}-\vec{q}\cdot\vec{q}^{\prime}))\,,

(D.26)

\displaystyle\vec{q}\cdot\vec{q}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=rr^{\prime}(\cos(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\sin(\phi)\sin(\phi^{\prime})+\cos(\phi)\cos(\phi^{\prime}))\,.

Together, these imply that the integral in brackets in Eq. (
D.25
) equals

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\cos(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\sin(\phi)\sin(\phi^{\prime})+\cos(\phi)\cos(\phi^{\prime})))

(D.27)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times G_{34}((q^{\prime})^{0}\alpha-r^{\prime}\delta\cos(\phi^{\prime}))\sin(\phi^{\prime})d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\cos(\psi)\sin(\phi)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}+\cos(\phi)y))G_{34}((q^{\prime})^{0}\alpha-r^{\prime}\delta y)d\psi dy\,.

Therefore

\displaystyle K(s,p)=

\displaystyle\frac{8\pi rr^{\prime}}{s}\int_{-1}^{1}\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\cos(\psi)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}\sqrt{1-z^{2}}+yz))d\psi\right)\right.

(D.28)

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97716pt\times G_{34}((q^{\prime})^{0}\alpha-r^{\prime}\delta y)dy\bigg]G_{12}(q^{0}\alpha-r\delta z)dz\,,

where

\displaystyle t(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}((q^{0})^{2}-r^{2}+((q^{\prime})^{0})^{2}-(r^{\prime})^{2}-2q^{0}(q^{\prime})^{0}+2rr^{\prime}x)=\frac{1}{4}((q^{0}-(q^{\prime})^{0})^{2}-r^{2}-(r^{\prime})^{2}+2rr^{\prime}x)\,.

(D.29)

D.2
Electron and neutrino collision integrals

In this section, we further simplify the various integrals of the scattering matrix element that appear in the scattering kernels for processes involving
e^{\pm}
and neutrinos. For reference, we collect the important prior results
on evaluation of the scattering kernel integrals Eq. (
D.1
), where we have changed notation from
|\vec{p}|
to
p
.

\displaystyle M=\frac{1}{256(2\pi)^{7}}\int_{s_{0}}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}K(s,p)\frac{p^{2}}{p^{0}}dpds\,,

(D.30)

\displaystyle K(s,p)=

\displaystyle\frac{8\pi rr^{\prime}}{s}\int_{-1}^{1}\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\cos(\psi)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}\sqrt{1-z^{2}}+yz))d\psi\right)\right.

(D.31)

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97716pt\times G_{34}((q^{\prime})^{0}\alpha-r^{\prime}\delta y)dy\bigg]G_{12}(q^{0}\alpha-r\delta z)dz\,,

where

\displaystyle p^{0}=

\displaystyle\sqrt{p^{2}+s}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt\alpha=\frac{p^{0}}{\sqrt{s}}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt\delta=\frac{p}{\sqrt{s}}\,,\hskip 5.69054ptq^{0}=\frac{m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt(q^{\prime})^{0}=\frac{m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\,,

(D.32)

\displaystyle r=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}\sqrt{(s-(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2})(s-(m_{1}-m_{2})^{2})}\,,

\displaystyle r^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}\sqrt{(s-(m_{3}+m_{4})^{2})(s-(m_{3}-m_{4})^{2})}\,,

\displaystyle t(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}((q^{0}-(q^{\prime})^{0})^{2}-r^{2}-(r^{\prime})^{2}+2rr^{\prime}x)\,,

\displaystyle s_{0}=

\displaystyle\max\{(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2},(m_{3}+m_{4})^{2}\}\,,

and

\displaystyle F(p,q,q^{\prime})=

\displaystyle F_{12}((p+q)\cdot U/2,(p-q)\cdot U/2)F_{34}((p+q^{\prime})\cdot U/2,(p-q^{\prime})\cdot U/2)

(D.33)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle G_{12}(p\cdot U,q\cdot U)G_{34}(p\cdot U,q^{\prime}\cdot U)\,.

This is as far as we can simplify the collision integrals without more information about the form of the matrix elements. The matrix elements for weak force scattering processes involving neutrinos and
e^{\pm}
in the limit
|p|\ll M_{W},M_{Z}
, taken from
[
308
,
309
]
, are as follows

Process

S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}

\nu_{e}+\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{e}+\bar{\nu}_{e}

128G_{F}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})

\nu_{e}+\nu_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{e}+\nu_{e}

64G_{F}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})(p_{3}\cdot p_{4})

\nu_{e}+\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{j}+\bar{\nu}_{j}

32G_{F}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})

\nu_{e}+\bar{\nu}_{j}\rightarrow\nu_{e}+\bar{\nu}_{j}

32G_{F}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})

\nu_{e}+\nu_{j}\rightarrow\nu_{e}+\nu_{j}

32G_{F}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})(p_{3}\cdot p_{4})

\nu_{e}+\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow e^{+}+e^{-}

128G_{F}^{2}[g_{L}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})+g_{R}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{3})(p_{2}\cdot p_{4})+g_{L}g_{R}m_{e}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})]

\nu_{e}+e^{-}\rightarrow\nu_{e}+e^{-}

128G_{F}^{2}[g_{L}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})(p_{3}\cdot p_{4})+g_{R}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})-g_{L}g_{R}m_{e}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{3})]

\nu_{e}+e^{+}\rightarrow\nu_{e}+e^{+}

128G_{F}^{2}[g_{R}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})(p_{3}\cdot p_{4})+g_{L}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})-g_{L}g_{R}m_{e}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{3})]

Table 8:
Matrix elements for electron neutrino processes where
j=\mu,\tau
,
g_{L}=\frac{1}{2}+\sin^{2}\theta_{W}
,
g_{R}=\sin^{2}\theta_{W}
,
\sin^{2}(\theta_{W})\approx 0.23
is the Weinberg angle, and
G_{F}=1.16637\times 10^{-5}\text{GeV}^{-2}
is Fermi’s constant.

Process

S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}

\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{i}\rightarrow\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{i}

128G_{F}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})

\nu_{i}+\nu_{i}\rightarrow\nu_{i}+\nu_{i}

64G_{F}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})(p_{3}\cdot p_{4})

\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{i}\rightarrow\nu_{j}+\bar{\nu}_{j}

32G_{F}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})

\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{j}\rightarrow\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{j}

32G_{F}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})

\nu_{i}+\nu_{j}\rightarrow\nu_{i}+\nu_{j}

32G_{F}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})(p_{3}\cdot p_{4})

\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{i}\rightarrow e^{+}+e^{-}

128G_{F}^{2}[\tilde{g}_{L}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})+g_{R}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{3})(p_{2}\cdot p_{4})+\tilde{g}_{L}g_{R}m_{e}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})]

\nu_{i}+e^{-}\rightarrow\nu_{i}+e^{-}

128G_{F}^{2}[\tilde{g}_{L}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})(p_{3}\cdot p_{4})+g_{R}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})-\tilde{g}_{L}g_{R}m_{e}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{3})]

\nu_{i}+e^{+}\rightarrow\nu_{i}+e^{+}

128G_{F}^{2}[g_{R}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})(p_{3}\cdot p_{4})+\tilde{g}_{L}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3})-\tilde{g}_{L}g_{R}m_{e}^{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{3})]

Table 9:
Matrix elements for
\mu
and
\tau
neutrino processes where
i=\mu,\tau
,
j=e,\mu,\tau
,
j\neq i
,
\tilde{g}_{L}=g_{L}-1=-\frac{1}{2}+\sin^{2}\theta_{W}
,
g_{R}=\sin^{2}\theta_{W}
,
\sin^{2}(\theta_{W})\approx 0.23
is the Weinberg angle, and
G_{F}=1.16637\times 10^{-5}\text{GeV}^{-2}
is Fermi’s constant.

In the following subsections, we will analytically simplify Eq. (
D.30
) for each of these processes.

Neutrino-neutrino scattering

Using Eq. (
C.16
), the matrix elements for neutrino-neutrino scattering
\nu\nu\rightarrow\nu\nu
can be simplified to

\displaystyle S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}=C(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})(p_{3}\cdot p_{4})=C\frac{s^{2}}{4}\,,

(D.34)

where the coefficient
C
is given in table
10
.

Process

C

\nu_{i}+\nu_{i}\rightarrow\nu_{i}+\nu_{i},\hskip 5.69054pti\in\{e,\mu,\tau\}

64G_{F}^{2}

\nu_{i}+\nu_{j}\rightarrow\nu_{i}+\nu_{j},\hskip 5.69054pti\neq j,\hskip 2.84526pti,j\in\{e,\mu,\tau\}

32G_{F}^{2}

Table 10:
Matrix element coefficients for neutrino neutrino scattering processes.

From here we obtain

\displaystyle K(s,p)=

\displaystyle\frac{8\pi rr^{\prime}}{s}\int_{-1}^{1}\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\cos(\psi)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}\sqrt{1-z^{2}}+yz))d\psi\right)\right.

(D.35)

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97716pt\times G_{34}(p^{0},(q^{\prime})^{0}\alpha-r^{\prime}\delta y)dy\bigg]G_{12}(p^{0},q^{0}\alpha-r\delta z)dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi^{2}Crr^{\prime}s\int_{-1}^{1}G_{12}(p^{0},q^{0}\alpha-r\delta z)dz\int_{-1}^{1}G_{34}(p^{0},(q^{\prime})^{0}\alpha-r^{\prime}\delta y)dy\,.

Therefore

\displaystyle M_{\nu\nu\rightarrow\nu\nu}=

\displaystyle\frac{C}{256(2\pi)^{5}}T^{8}\!\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\tilde{s}^{2}\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\tilde{G}_{12}(\tilde{p}^{0},-\tilde{p}z)dz\int_{-1}^{1}\tilde{G}_{34}(\tilde{p}^{0},-\tilde{p}y)dy\right]\frac{\tilde{p}^{2}}{\tilde{p}^{0}}d\tilde{p}d\tilde{s}\,,

(D.36)

where the tilde quantities are obtained by non-dimensionalizing via scaling by
T
and we have re-introduced the dependence of
G_{i,j}
on
p^{0}
. If we want to emphasize the role of
C
then we write
M_{\nu\nu\rightarrow\nu\nu}(C)
.

), the matrix elements for neutrino antineutrino scattering
\nu\bar{\nu}\rightarrow\nu\bar{\nu}
can be simplified to

\displaystyle S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}=C\left(\frac{s+t}{2}\right)^{2}\,,

(D.37)

where the coefficient
C
is given in table
11
.

Process

C

\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{i}\rightarrow\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{i},\hskip 5.69054pti\in\{e,\mu,\tau\}

128G_{F}^{2}

\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{i}\rightarrow\nu_{j}+\bar{\nu}_{j},\hskip 5.69054pti\neq j,\hskip 2.84526pti,j\in\{e,\mu,\tau\}

\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{j}\rightarrow\nu_{i}+\bar{\nu}_{j},\hskip 5.69054pti\neq j,\hskip 2.84526pti,j\in\{e,\mu,\tau\}

Using this we find

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\cos(\psi)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}\sqrt{1-z^{2}}+yz))d\psi=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi C}{16}s^{2}(3+4yz-y^{2}-z^{2}+3y^{2}z^{2})\equiv\frac{\pi C}{16}s^{2}q(y,z)\,,

(D.38)

\displaystyle K(s,p)=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}C}{2}s^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\left[\int_{-1}^{1}q(y,z)G_{34}(p^{0},-py)dy\right]G_{12}(p^{0},-pz)dz\,.

Therefore

\displaystyle M_{\nu\bar{\nu}\rightarrow\nu\bar{\nu}}=\frac{C}{2048(2\pi)^{5}}T^{8}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\tilde{s}^{2}\bigg[\int_{-1}^{1}\!\int_{-1}^{1}q(y,z)\tilde{G}_{34}(\tilde{p}^{0},-\tilde{p}y)\tilde{G}_{12}(\tilde{p}^{0},-\tilde{p}z)dydz\bigg]\frac{\tilde{p}^{2}}{\tilde{p}^{0}}d\tilde{p}d\tilde{s}\,.

(D.39)

If we want to emphasize the role of
C
then we write
M_{\nu\bar{\nu}\rightarrow\nu\bar{\nu}}(C)
. Note that due to the polynomial form of the matrix element integral, the double integral in brackets breaks into a linear combination of products of one dimensional integrals, meaning that the nesting of integrals is again only three deep in practice.

.

The integral of each of these terms is

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{(s+t(\psi)-m_{e}^{2})^{2}}{4}d\psi=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{16}s(3s-4m_{e}^{2})+\frac{\pi}{4}s^{3/2}\sqrt{s-4m_{e}^{2}}yz-\frac{\pi}{16}s(s-4m_{e}^{2})(y^{2}+z^{2})+\frac{3\pi}{16}s(s-4m_{e}^{2})y^{2}z^{2}\,,

(D.41)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{(m_{e}^{2}-t(\psi))^{2}}{4}d\psi=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{16}s(3s-4m_{e}^{2})-\frac{\pi}{4}s^{3/2}\sqrt{s-4m_{e}^{2}}yz-\frac{\pi}{16}s(s-4m_{e}^{2})(y^{2}+z^{2})+\frac{3\pi}{16}s(s-4m_{e}^{2})y^{2}z^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}m_{e}^{2}\frac{s}{2}d\psi=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\psi))d\psi=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{16}s[3s(A+B)+4m_{e}^{2}(4C-A-B)]+\frac{\pi}{4}s^{3/2}\sqrt{s-4m_{e}^{2}}(A-B)yz

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi}{16}s(s-4m_{e}^{2})(A+B)(y^{2}+z^{2})+\frac{3\pi}{16}s(s-4m_{e}^{2})(A+B)y^{2}z^{2}\equiv\pi q(m_{e},s,y,z)\,,

and hence

\displaystyle M_{\nu\bar{\nu}\rightarrow e^{+}e^{-}}

(D.43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{128(2\pi)^{5}}\int_{4m_{e}^{2}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\sqrt{1-4m_{e}^{2}/s}\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}q(s,y,z,m_{e})G_{34}(p^{0},-(\sqrt{1-4m_{e}^{2}/s})py)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 193.47873pt\times G_{12}(p^{0},-pz)dydz\bigg]\frac{p^{2}}{p^{0}}dpds,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{T^{8}}{128(2\pi)^{5}}\int_{4\tilde{m}_{e}^{2}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\sqrt{1-4\tilde{m}_{e}^{2}/\tilde{s}}\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}q(\tilde{s},y,z,\tilde{m}_{e})\tilde{G}_{34}(\tilde{p}^{0},-(\sqrt{1-4\tilde{m}_{e}^{2}/\tilde{s}})\tilde{p}y)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 193.47873pt\times\tilde{G}_{12}(\tilde{p}^{0},-\tilde{p}z)dydz\bigg]\frac{\tilde{p}^{2}}{\tilde{p}^{0}}d\tilde{p}d\tilde{s}\,,

where
\tilde{m_{e}}=m_{e}/T
. If we want to emphasize the role of
A,B,C
then we write
M_{\nu\bar{\nu}\rightarrow e^{+}e^{-}}(A,B,C)
. Note that this expression is linear in
(A,B,C)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Also note that, under our assumptions that the distributions of
e^{+}
and
e^{-}
are the same, the
G_{ij}
terms that contain the product of
e^{\pm}
distributions are even functions. Hence the term involving the integral of
yz
vanishes by antisymmetry.

.

The integral of each of these terms is

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{(s-m_{e}^{2})^{2}}{4}d\psi=\pi\frac{(s-m_{e}^{2})^{2}}{2},

(D.45)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{(s+t(\psi)-m_{e}^{2})^{2}}{4}d\psi=\frac{\pi}{16s^{2}}(s-m_{e}^{2})^{2}(3m_{e}^{4}+2m_{e}^{2}s+3s^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\pi}{4s^{2}}(s-m_{e}^{2})^{3}(s+m_{e}^{2})yz-\frac{\pi}{16s^{2}}(s-m_{e}^{2})^{4}(y^{2}+z^{2})+\frac{3\pi}{16s^{2}}(s-m_{e}^{2})^{4}y^{2}z^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}m_{e}^{2}\frac{t(\psi)}{2}d\psi=-\frac{\pi}{2s}m_{e}^{2}(s-m_{e}^{2})^{2}(1-yz)\,.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\psi))d\psi=

\displaystyle\pi\left[\frac{A}{2}+\frac{B}{16s^{2}}(3m_{e}^{4}+2m_{e}^{2}s+3s^{2})-\frac{C}{2s}m_{e}^{2}\right](s-m_{e}^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle+\pi\left[\frac{B}{4s^{2}}(s-m_{e}^{2})(s+m_{e}^{2})+\frac{C}{2s}m_{e}^{2}\right](s-m_{e}^{2})^{2}yz

\displaystyle-B\frac{\pi}{16s^{2}}(s-m_{e}^{2})^{4}(y^{2}+z^{2})+B\frac{3\pi}{16s^{2}}(s-m_{e}^{2})^{4}y^{2}z^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\pi q(m_{e},s,y,z)

(D.46)

and

\displaystyle K(s,p)=

\displaystyle\frac{8\pi^{2}rr^{\prime}}{s}\!\!\int_{-1}^{1}\!\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\!q(m_{e},s,y,z)G_{34}(p^{0},(q^{\prime})^{0}\alpha-r^{\prime}\delta y)dy\right]\!G_{12}(p^{0},q^{0}\alpha-r\delta z)dz\,,

(D.47)

\displaystyle r=r^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\frac{s-m_{e}^{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\,,\hskip 5.69054ptq^{0}=(q^{\prime})^{0}=-\frac{m_{e}^{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt\delta=\frac{p}{\sqrt{s}}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt\alpha=\frac{p^{0}}{\sqrt{s}}\,.

This implies

\displaystyle M_{\nu e\rightarrow\nu e}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{128(2\pi)^{5}}\int_{m_{e}^{2}}^{\infty}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}(1-m_{e}^{2}/s)^{2}\left(\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}q(m_{e},s,y,z)G_{34}(p^{0},(q^{\prime})^{0}\alpha-r^{\prime}\delta y)\right.

(D.48)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times G_{12}(p^{0},q^{0}\alpha-r\delta z)dydz\bigg)\frac{p^{2}}{p^{0}}dpds\,.

As above, after scaling all masses by
T
, we obtain a prefactor of
T^{8}
. If we want to emphasize the role of
A,B,C
then we write
M_{\nu e\rightarrow\nu e}(A,B,C)
. Note that this expression is also linear in
(A,B,C)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Total collision integral

We now give the total collision integrals for neutrinos. In the following, we indicate which distributions are used in each of the four types of scattering integrals discussed above by using the appropriate subscripts. For example, to compute
M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}
we set
G_{1,2}=\hat{\psi}_{j}f^{1}f^{2}
,
G_{3,4}=f_{3}f_{4}
,
f_{1}=f_{\nu_{e}}
,
f_{3}=f_{\nu_{e}}
, and
f_{2}=f_{4}=f_{\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}
in the expression Eq. (
D.39
) for
M_{\nu\bar{\nu}\rightarrow\nu\bar{\nu}}
and then, to include the reverse direction of the process, we must
s
ubtract the analogous expression whose only difference is
G_{1,2}=\hat{\psi}_{j}f_{1}f_{2}
,
G_{3,4}=f^{3}f^{4}
.
With this notation the collision integral for
\nu_{e}
is

\displaystyle M_{\nu_{e}}=

\displaystyle[M_{\nu_{e}\nu_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\nu_{e}}+M_{\nu_{e}\nu_{\mu}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\nu_{\mu}}+M_{\nu_{e}\nu_{\tau}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\nu_{\tau}}]

(D.49)

\displaystyle+[M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{e}}+M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{\mu}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}+M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{\tau}\bar{\nu}_{\tau}}+M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}+M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{\tau}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{\tau}}]

\displaystyle+M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow e^{+}e^{-}}+[M_{\nu_{e}e^{-}\rightarrow\nu_{e}e^{-}}+M_{\nu_{e}e^{+}\rightarrow\nu_{e}e^{+}}]\,.

Symmetry among the interactions implies that the distributions of
\nu_{\mu}
and
\nu_{\tau}
are equal. We also neglect the small matter anti-matter asymmetry and so we take the distribution of each particle to be equal to that of the corresponding antiparticle. Therefore there are only three independent distributions,
f_{\nu_{e}}
,
f_{\nu_{\mu}}
, and
f_{e}
. This allows us to combine several of the terms in Eq. (
D.49
) to obtain

\displaystyle M_{\nu_{e}}=

\displaystyle M_{\nu_{e}\nu_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\nu_{e}}(64G_{F}^{2})+M_{\nu_{e}\nu_{\mu}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\nu_{\mu}}(2\times 32G_{F}^{2})+M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{e}}(128G_{F}^{2})

(D.50)

\displaystyle+M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{\mu}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}(2\times 32G_{F}^{2})+M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}(2\times 32G_{F}^{2})

\displaystyle+M_{\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow e^{+}e^{-}}(128G_{F}^{2}g_{L}^{2},128G_{F}^{2}g_{R}^{2},128G_{F}^{2}g_{L}g_{R})

\displaystyle+M_{\nu_{e}e\rightarrow\nu_{e}e}(128G_{F}^{2}(g_{L}^{2}+g_{R}^{2}),128G_{F}^{2}(g_{L}^{2}+g_{R}^{2}),256G_{F}^{2}g_{L}g_{R})\,.

Introducing one more piece of notation, we use a subscript
k
to denote the orthogonal polynomial basis element that multiplies
f_{1}
or
f^{1}
in the inner product. The inner product of the
k
th basis element with the total scattering operator for electron neutrinos is therefore

\displaystyle R_{k}=

\displaystyle 2\pi^{2}T^{-3}M_{k,\nu_{e}}\,.

(D.51)

Under these same assumptions and conventions, the total collision integral for the combined
\nu_{\mu}
,
\nu_{\tau}
distribution (which we label
\nu_{\mu}
) is

\displaystyle M_{\nu_{\mu}}=

\displaystyle M_{\nu_{\mu}\nu_{\mu}\rightarrow\nu_{\mu}\nu_{\mu}}(64G_{F}^{2}+32G_{F}^{2})+M_{\nu_{\mu}\nu_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{\mu}\nu_{e}}(32G_{F}^{2})

(D.52)

\displaystyle+M_{\nu_{\mu}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}\rightarrow\nu_{\mu}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}(128G_{F}^{2}+32G_{F}^{2}+32G_{F}^{2})

\displaystyle+M_{\nu_{\mu}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}\rightarrow\nu_{e}\bar{\nu}_{e}}(32G_{F}^{2})+M_{\nu_{\mu}\bar{\nu}_{e}\rightarrow\nu_{\mu}\bar{\nu}_{e}}(32G_{F}^{2})

\displaystyle+M_{\nu_{\mu}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}\rightarrow e^{+}e^{-}}(128G_{F}^{2}\tilde{g}_{L}^{2},128G_{F}^{2}g_{R}^{2},128G_{F}^{2}\tilde{g}_{L}g_{R})

\displaystyle+M_{\nu_{\mu}e\rightarrow\nu_{\mu}e}(128G_{F}^{2}(\tilde{g}_{L}^{2}+g_{R}^{2}),128G_{F}^{2}(\tilde{g}_{L}^{2}+g_{R}^{2}),256G_{F}^{2}\tilde{g}_{L}g_{R})\,,

\displaystyle R_{k}=

\displaystyle 2\pi^{2}T^{-3}M_{k,\nu_{\mu}}\,.

(D.53)

Neutrino freeze-out test

Now that we have the above expressions for the neutrino scattering integrals, we can compare the chemical equilibrium and nonequilibrium methods on the problem of neutrino freeze-out using the full
2
-
2
scattering kernels for neutrino processes. We solve the Boltzmann-Einstein equation, Eq. (
6.45
), for both the electron neutrino distribution and the combined
\mu
,
\tau
neutrino distribution, including all of the processes outlined above in the scattering operator, together with the Hubble equation for
a(t)
, Eq. (
1.5
). The total energy density appearing in the Hubble equation consists of the contributions from both independent neutrino distributions as well as chemical equilibrium
e^{\pm}
and photon distributions at some common temperature
T_{\gamma}
, all computed using Eq. (
C.18
). The dynamics of
T_{\gamma}
are fixed by the divergence freedom condition of the total stress energy tensor implied by Einstein’s equations. In addition, we include the QED corrections to the
e^{\pm}
and photon equations of state from Sec.
3.4
.

To compare our results with Ref.
[
29
]
, where neutrino freeze-out was simulated using
\sin^{2}(\theta_{W})=0.23
and
\eta=\eta_{0}
, in table
14
we present
N_{\nu}
together with the following quantities

\displaystyle z_{fin}=T_{\gamma}a,\hskip 5.69054pt\rho_{\nu 0}=\frac{7}{120}\pi^{2}a^{-4},\hskip 5.69054pt\delta\bar{\rho}_{\nu}=\frac{\rho_{\nu}}{\rho_{\nu 0}}-1.

(D.54)

This quantities were introduced in Ref.
[
29
]
, but some additional discussion of their significance is in order. The normalization of the scale factor
a
is chosen so that at the start of the computation
T_{\gamma}=1/a
. This means that
1/a
is the temperature of a (hypothetical) particle species that is completely decoupled throughout the computation. Here we will call it the free-streaming temperature.
z_{fin}
is the ratio of photon temperature to the free-streaming temperature. It is a measure of the amount of reheating that photons underwent due to the annihilation of
e^{\pm}
. For completely decoupled neutrinos, whose temperature is the free-streaming temperature, the well known value can be computed from conservation of entropy

z_{fin}=(11/4)^{1/3}\approx 1.401.

(D.55)

For coupled neutrinos, one expects this value to be slightly reduced, due to the transfer of some entropy from annihilating
e^{\pm}
into neutrinos. This is reflected in Table
14
.

\rho_{\nu 0}
is the energy density of a massless fermion with two degrees of freedom and temperature equal to the free-streaming temperature. In other words, it is the energy density of a single neutrino species, assuming it decoupled before reheating. Consequently,
\delta\bar{\rho}_{\nu}
is the fractional increase in the energy density of a coupled neutrino species, due to its participation in reheating.

We compute the above using both the chemical equilibrium and nonequilibrium methods. For the following results, we used
\sin^{2}(\theta_{W})=0.23
and
\eta=\eta_{0}
.

Method

Modes

z_{fin}

\delta\bar{\rho}_{\nu_{e}}

\delta\bar{\rho}_{\nu_{\mu,\tau}}

N_{\nu}

Chemical Eq

4

1.39785

0.009230

0.003792

3.044269

Chemical Non-Eq

2

1.39784

0.009269

0.003799

3.044383

Chemical Non-Eq

3

1.39785

0.009230

0.003791

3.044264

Table 14:
Equilibrium and nonequilibrium values for relevant parameters in different modes.

We see that
\Delta N_{\nu}\equiv N_{\nu}-3
agrees to
2
digits and
4
digits when using
2
and
3
modes respectively for the chemical nonequilibrium method, and similar behavior holds for the other quantities. Due to the reduction in the required number of modes, the chemical nonequilibrium method with the minimum number of required modes (
2
modes) is more than
20\times
faster than the chemical equilibrium method with its minimum number of required modes (
4
modes), a very significant speed-up when the minimum number of modes meets the required precision. The value of
N_{\nu}
we obtain agrees with that found by
[
29
]
, up to their cited error tolerance of
\pm 0.002
.

Conservation laws and scattering integrals

For some processes, various of the
R_{k}
’s vanish exactly, as we now show. First consider processes in which
f_{1}=f_{3}
and
f_{2}=f_{4}
, such as in kinetic scattering processes. Since
m_{1}=m_{3}
and
m_{2}=m_{4}
we have
r=r^{\prime}
,
q^{0}=(q^{\prime})^{0}
. The scattering terms are all two dimensional integrals of some function of
s
and
p
multiplied by the quantity

\displaystyle I_{k}\equiv\int_{-1}^{1}\!\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\!S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\cos(\psi)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}\sqrt{1-z^{2}}+yz))d\psi f_{1}(h_{1}(y))f_{2}(h_{2}(y))dy\right]

(D.56)

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97716pt\times f_{k}^{1}(h_{1}(z))f^{2}(h_{2}(z))dz

\displaystyle-\int_{-1}^{1}\!\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\!S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\cos(\psi)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}\sqrt{1-z^{2}}+yz))d\psi f^{1}(h_{1}(y))f^{2}(h_{2}(y))dy\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97716pt\times f_{1,k}(h_{1}(z))f_{2}(h_{2}(z))dz\,,

\displaystyle h_{1}(y)=(p^{0}+(q^{\prime})^{0}\alpha-r^{\prime}\delta y)/2\,,\hskip 5.69054pth_{2}(y)=(p^{0}-q^{0}\alpha+r\delta y)/2\,,

\displaystyle f_{1,k}=\hat{\psi}_{k}f_{1}\,,f^{1}_{k}=\hat{\psi}_{k}f^{1}\,.

Note that for
k=0
,
\hat{\psi}_{0}
is constant. After factoring it out of
I_{k}
, the result is clearly zero and so
R_{0}=0
.

We further specialize to a distribution scattering from itself i.e.
f_{1}=f_{2}=f_{3}=f_{4}
. Since
m_{1}=m_{2}
and
m_{3}=m_{4}
we have
q^{0}=(q^{\prime})^{0}=0
and

h_{1}(y)=(p^{0}-r^{\prime}\delta y)/2,\hskip 5.69054pth_{2}(y)=(p^{0}+r\delta y)/2\,.

(D.57)

By the above, we know that
R_{0}=0
.
\hat{\psi}_{1}
appears in
I_{1}
in the form
\hat{\psi}_{1}(h_{1}(z))
, a degree one polynomial in
z
. Therefore
R_{1}
is a sum of two terms, one which comes from the degree zero part and one from the degree one part. The former is zero, again by the above reasoning. Therefore, to show that
R_{1}=0
we need only show
I_{1}=0
, except with
\hat{\psi}_{1}(h_{1}(z))
replaced by
z
. Since
h_{1}(-y)=h_{2}(y)
, changing variables
y\rightarrow-y
and
z\rightarrow-z
in the following shows that this term is equal to its own negative, and hence is zero

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\cos(\psi)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}\sqrt{1-z^{2}}+yz))d\psi f_{1}(h_{1}(y))f_{1}(h_{2}(y))dy\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97716pt\times zf^{1}(h_{1}(z))f^{1}(h_{2}(z))dz

(D.58)

\displaystyle-\int_{-1}^{1}\left[\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t(\cos(\psi)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}\sqrt{1-z^{2}}+yz))d\psi f^{1}(h_{1}(y))f^{1}(h_{2}(y))dy\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97716pt\times zf_{1}(h_{1}(z))f_{1}(h_{2}(z))dz\,.

We note that the corresponding scattering integrals do not vanish for the chemical equilibrium spectral method. This is another advantage of the method developed in Appendix
C
and leads to a further reduction in cost of the method, beyond just the reduction in minimum number of modes.

Finally, we point out how the vanishing of these inner products is a reflection of certain conservation laws. From Eq. (
C.22
), Eq. (
D.1
), and the fact that
\hat{\psi}_{0},\hat{\psi}_{1}
span the space of polynomials of degree
\leq 1
, we have the following expressions for the change in number density and energy density of a massless particle

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a^{3}}\frac{d}{dt}(a^{3}n)=\frac{g_{p}}{2\pi^{2}}\int\frac{1}{E}C[f]p^{2}dp=c_{0}R_{0}\,,\qquad\frac{1}{a^{4}}\frac{d}{dt}(a^{4}\rho)=\frac{g_{p}}{2\pi^{2}}\int C[f]p^{2}dp=d_{0}R_{0}+d_{1}R_{1}\,,

(D.59)

for some
c_{0},d_{0},d_{1}
. Therefore, the vanishing of
R_{0}
is equivalent to conservation of comoving particle number. The vanishing of
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
implies
\rho\propto 1/a^{4}
i.e. that the reduction in energy density is due entirely to redshift; energy is not lost from the distribution due to scattering. These findings match the situations above where we found one or both of
R_{0}=0
,
R_{1}=0
.
R_{0}
vanished for all kinetic scattering processes and we know that all such processes conserve comoving particle number. Both
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
vanished for a distribution scattering from itself and in such a process there is no energy loss energy from the distribution by scattering; energy is only redistributed among the particles corresponding to that distribution.

D.3
Comparison with an alternative method for computing scattering integrals

As a comparison and consistency check for our method of computing the scattering integrals, in this appendix we analytically reduce the collision integral down to
3
dimensions by a method adapted from
[
308
,
309
]
. The only difference between our treatment in this section and theirs being that they solved the Boltzmann equation numerically on a grid in momentum space and not via a spectral method. Therefore we must take an inner product of the collision operator with a basis function and hence we are integrating over all particle momenta, whereas they integrate over all momenta except that of particle one. For completeness we give a detailed discussion of their method.

Writing the conservation of four-momentum enforcing delta function

\delta(\Delta p)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\delta(\Delta E)e^{i\vec{z}\cdot\Delta\vec{p}}d^{3}z\,,

(D.60)

where the arrow denoted the spatial component, we can simplify the collision integral as follows

\displaystyle R\equiv

\displaystyle\int G(E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},E_{4})S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t)(2\pi)^{4}\delta(\Delta p)\prod_{i=1}^{4}\frac{d^{3}p_{i}}{2(2\pi)^{3}E_{i}}

(D.61)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{16(2\pi)^{11}}\int G(E_{i})S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t)\delta(\Delta E)e^{i\vec{z}\cdot\Delta p}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\frac{d^{3}p_{i}}{E_{i}}d^{3}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{6}}\int G(E_{i})K(E_{i})\delta(\Delta E)\prod_{i=1}^{4}\frac{p_{i}}{E_{i}}dp_{i}z^{2}dz\,,

\displaystyle K\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}p_{4}}{(4\pi)^{5}}\int S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t)e^{i\vec{z}\cdot\Delta\vec{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}d\Omega_{i}d\Omega_{z}\,.

(D.62)

We can change variables from
p_{i}
to
E_{i}
in the outer integrals and use the delta function to eliminate the integration over
E_{4}
to obtain

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{6}}\int 1_{E_{1}+E_{2}-E_{3}>m_{4}}G(E_{i})\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}K(z,E_{i})z^{2}dz\right]dE_{1}dE_{2}dE_{3}\,,

(D.63)

\displaystyle p_{i}=

\displaystyle\sqrt{E_{i}^{2}-m_{i}^{2}},\hskip 5.69054ptE_{4}=E_{1}+E_{2}-E_{3}\,.

From Tables
8
and
9
we see that the matrix elements for weak scattering involving neutrinos are linear combinations of the terms

p_{1}\cdot p_{2},\hskip 5.69054ptp_{1}\cdot p_{3},\hskip 5.69054pt(p_{1}\cdot p_{4})(p_{2}\cdot p_{3}),\hskip 5.69054pt(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})(p_{3}\cdot p_{4}),\hskip 5.69054pt(p_{1}\cdot p_{3})(p_{2}\cdot p_{4}).

(D.64)

Therefore we must compute the angular integral term
K
with
S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}
replaced by elements from the following list

\displaystyle 1,\hskip 5.69054pt\vec{p}_{1}\cdot\vec{p}_{2},\hskip 5.69054pt\vec{p}_{1}\cdot\vec{p}_{3},\hskip 5.69054pt\vec{p}_{1}\cdot\vec{p}_{4}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{p}_{3},\hskip 5.69054pt\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{p}_{4}\,,\hskip 5.69054pt\vec{p}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{4}\,,

(D.65)

\displaystyle(\vec{p}_{1}\cdot\vec{p}_{2})(\vec{p}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{4}),\hskip 5.69054pt(\vec{p}_{1}\cdot\vec{p}_{4})(\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}),\hskip 5.69054pt(\vec{p}_{1}\cdot\vec{p}_{3})(\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{p}_{4})\,,

producing
K_{0}
,
K_{12}
,
K_{13}
,…,
K_{1324}
. All of these are rotationally invariant, and so we can always rotate coordinates so that
\vec{z}=z\hat{z}
. This allows us to evaluate the
z
angular integral

K=\frac{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}p_{4}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\int S|\mathcal{M}|^{2}(s,t)e^{iz\hat{z}\cdot\Delta\vec{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{4}d\Omega_{i}\,.

(D.66)

The remaining angular integrals are straightforward to evaluate analytically for each expression in Eq. (
D.65
)

\displaystyle K_{0}=\prod_{i=1}^{4}\frac{\sin(p_{i}z)}{z}\,,

(D.67)

\displaystyle K_{12}=-\frac{(\sin(p_{1}z)-p_{1}z\cos(p_{1}z))(\sin(p_{2}z)-p_{2}z\cos(p_{2}z))\sin(p_{3}z)\sin(p_{4}z)}{z^{6}}\,,

\displaystyle K_{13}=\frac{(\sin(p_{1}z)-p_{1}z\cos(p_{1}z))\sin(p_{2}z)(\sin(p_{3}z)-p_{3}z\cos(p_{3}z))\sin(p_{4}z)}{z^{6}}\,,

\displaystyle K_{14}=\frac{(\sin(p_{1}z)-p_{1}z\cos(p_{1}z))\sin(p_{2}z)\sin(p_{3}z)(\sin(p_{4}z)-p_{4}z\cos(p_{4}z))}{z^{6}}\,,

\displaystyle K_{23}=\frac{\sin(p_{1}z)(\sin(p_{2}z)-p_{2}z\cos(p_{2}z))(\sin(p_{3}z)-p_{3}z\cos(p_{3}z))\sin(p_{4}z)}{z^{6}}\,,

\displaystyle K_{24}=\frac{\sin(p_{1}z)(\sin(p_{2}z)-p_{2}z\cos(p_{2}z))\sin(p_{3}z)(\sin(p_{4}z)-p_{4}z\cos(p_{4}z))}{z^{6}}\,,

\displaystyle K_{34}=-\frac{\sin(p_{1}z)\sin(p_{2}z)(\sin(p_{3}z)-p_{3}z\cos(p_{3}z))(\sin(p_{4}z)-p_{4}z\cos(p_{4}z))}{z^{6}}\,,

\displaystyle K_{1234}=K_{1423}=K_{1324}=\prod_{i=1}^{4}\frac{(\sin(p_{i}z)-p_{i}z\cos(p_{i}z))}{z^{2}}\,.

To compute
\int_{0}^{\infty}K(z)z^{2}dz
we need to evaluate the following three integrals

\displaystyle D_{1}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin(p_{1}z)\sin(p_{2}z)\sin(p_{3}z)\sin(p_{4}z)}{z^{2}}dz\,,

(D.68)

\displaystyle D_{2}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin(p_{1}z)\sin(p_{2}z)(\sin(p_{3}z)-p_{3}z\cos(p_{3}z))(\sin(p_{4}z)-p_{4}z\cos(p_{4}z))}{z^{4}}dz\,,

\displaystyle D_{3}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}(\sin(p_{i}z)-p_{i}z\cos(p_{i}z))}{z^{6}}dz\,.

These expressions are symmetric under
1\leftrightarrow 2
and
3\leftrightarrow 4
and so without loss of generality we can assume
p_{1}\geq p_{2}
,
p_{3}\geq p_{4}
. We require
p_{1}\leq p_{2}+p_{3}+p_{4}
(and cyclic permutations) by conservation of energy. In the case where the above conditions all hold, we separate the computation into four additional cases in which the integrals can be evaluated analytically, as in
[
308
,
309
]
:

{\bf p_{1}+p_{2}>p_{3}+p_{4}\text{, \hskip 2.84526pt }p_{1}+p_{4}>p_{2}+p_{3}}
:

\displaystyle D_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{8}(p_{2}+p_{3}+p_{4}-p_{1})\,,\qquad D_{2}=\frac{\pi}{48}((p_{1}-p_{2})^{3}+2(p_{3}^{3}+p_{4}^{3})-3(p_{1}-p_{2})(p_{3}^{2}+p_{4}^{2})\,,

(D.69)

\displaystyle D_{3}=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{240}(p_{1}^{5}-p_{2}^{5}+5p_{2}^{3}(p_{3}^{2}+p_{4}^{2})-5p_{1}^{3}(p_{2}^{2}+p_{3}^{2}+p_{4}^{2})-(p_{3}+p_{4})^{3}(p_{3}^{2}-3p_{3}p_{4}+p_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 19.91692pt+5p_{2}^{2}(p_{3}^{3}+p_{4}^{3})+5p_{1}^{2}(p_{2}^{3}+p_{3}^{3}+p_{4}^{3}))\,.

{\bf p_{1}+p_{2}<p_{3}+p_{4}\text{, \hskip 2.84526pt }p_{1}+p_{4}>p_{2}+p_{3}}
:

\displaystyle D_{1}=\frac{\pi}{4}p_{2}\,,\qquad D_{2}=\frac{\pi}{24}p_{2}(3(p_{3}^{2}+p_{4}^{2}-p_{1}^{2})-p_{2}^{2})\,,\qquad D_{3}=\frac{\pi}{120}p_{2}^{3}(5(p_{1}^{2}+p_{3}^{2}+p_{4}^{2})-p_{2}^{2})\,.

(D.70)

{\bf p_{1}+p_{2}>p_{3}+p_{4}\text{, \hskip 2.84526pt }p_{1}+p_{4}<p_{2}+p_{3}}
:

\displaystyle D_{1}=\frac{\pi}{4}p_{4}\,,\qquad D_{2}=\frac{\pi}{12}p_{4}^{3}\,,\qquad D_{3}=\frac{\pi}{120}p_{4}^{3}(5(p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}+p_{3}^{2})-p_{4}^{2})\,.

(D.71)

{\bf p_{1}+p_{2}<p_{3}+p_{4}\text{, \hskip 2.84526pt }p_{1}+p_{4}<p_{2}+p_{3}}
:

\displaystyle D_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{8}(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{4}-p_{3})\,,\qquad D_{2}=\frac{\pi}{48}(-(p_{1}+p_{2})^{3}-2p_{3}^{3}+2p_{4}^{3}+3(p_{1}+p_{2})(p_{3}^{2}+p_{4}^{2}))\,,

(D.72)

\displaystyle D_{3}=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{240}(p_{3}^{5}-p_{4}^{5}-(p_{1}+p_{2})^{3}(p_{1}^{2}-3p_{1}p_{2}+p_{2}^{2})+5(p_{1}^{3}+p_{2}^{3})p_{3}^{2}-5(p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2})p_{3}^{3}

\displaystyle\hskip 19.91692pt+5(p_{1}^{3}+p_{2}^{3}-p_{3}^{3})p_{4}^{2}+5(p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}+p_{3}^{2})p_{4}^{3})\,.

We computed the remaining integrals numerically in several test cases for each of the reaction types in Sec.
D.2
and obtained agreement between this method and ours, up to the integration tolerance used. However, the method we have developed in this Appendix has the distinct advantage of resulting in smooth integrand. The expressions obtained here are only piecewise smooth and therefore much costlier to integrate numerically. Since the cost of numerically solving the Boltzmann equation is dominated by the cost of computing the collision integrals, we find that our approach constitutes a significant optimization in practice.
